Native vocabulary in the two KJV editions

Alpha and Omega

The earlier endin jelam used English end for the adjective “last.” The chart already gives hüŧh for “end.” Its adjective is hüŧh’na. Writing and a mark supply īvden. The speaker calls himself the first and last written signs of the Greek alphabet. The edition also keeps the two further pairs in Revelation 22:13.

I am Alpha and Omega, the beginning and the end, the first and the last.
Rôun neksus’t īvden oich hüŧh’na īvden pô oich hüŧh neksus’t oich hüŧh’na rēowya.

See the printed reading on page 22123

Ordinary words and names

Omibra, “wink,” joins eye and shut. Trembra, “fist,” names the closed hand. In Judges 14:17 the feast lasted, so the translation uses the verb for continuing. In Isaiah 14:8 the trees rejoice that the woodcutter has ceased coming up against them.

Yardēn names the Jordan and Dawīd names David. Roma’na expresses Roman affiliation or citizenship as the passage requires. The English source identifies the historical people, places and offices. Stable dictionary keys distinguish people who share a spoken name.
